l_{x}
, we have

\displaystyle\delta\Delta_{b}(9)=\frac{g^{2}\Delta_{f}\Delta_{b}}{8N}\int\frac{d\mathbf{k}_{\perp}d\mathbf{L}dl_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2d-2}}\frac{|\mathbf{L}|}{|l_{y}|\big[l_{y}^{4}+\mathbf{L}^{2}\big]\big[l_{y}^{2}+g^{2}B_{d}\frac{|\mathbf{L}|^{d-1}}{|l_{y}|}\big]},

where the integration is the same with
\delta\Delta_{b}(8)
. We may ignore the
l_{y}^{4}
term in the fermionic part since it would give rise to subleading terms in
g
. Integrating over
l_{y}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\delta\Delta_{b}(9)=\frac{g^{2/3}\Delta_{f}\Delta_{b}}{12\sqrt{3}B_{d}^{2/3}N}\int\frac{d\mathbf{k}_{\perp}d\mathbf{L}}{(2\pi)^{2d-3}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{L}|^{\frac{2d+1}{3}}}.

We drop this correction because it does not give a simple pole. As a result, we obtain

\delta\Delta_{b}(9)=0.

\displaystyle\delta\Delta_{b}(10)=-2\Delta_{f}^{2}\Delta_{b}\int\frac{d^{d}kd^{d}l}{(2\pi)^{2d}}\frac{\big[\delta_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{l}}\delta_{\mathbf{k}}+\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\cdot(\mathbf{k}_{\perp}+\mathbf{l}_{\perp})\big](\delta_{\mathbf{l}}\delta_{-\mathbf{l}}-\mathbf{l}_{\perp}^{2})+2l_{y}^{2}\big[\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{l}_{\perp}\delta_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{l}}-\mathbf{l}_{\perp}\cdot(\mathbf{k}_{\perp}+\mathbf{l}_{\perp})\delta_{\mathbf{k}}\big]}{\big[\delta_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{l}}^{2}+(\mathbf{k}_{\perp}+\mathbf{l}_{\perp})^{2}\big]\big[\delta_{\mathbf{k}}^{2}+\mathbf{k}_{\perp}^{2}\big]\big[\delta_{\mathbf{l}}^{2}+\mathbf{l}_{\perp}^{2}\big]\big[\delta_{-\mathbf{l}}^{2}+\mathbf{l}_{\perp}^{2}\big]}.

\displaystyle=-\Delta_{f}^{2}\Delta_{b}\int\frac{d\mathbf{k}_{\perp}dk_{y}d^{d}l}{(2\pi)^{2d-1}}\frac{1}{\big[(\delta_{\mathbf{l}}+2k_{y}l_{y})^{2}+(|\mathbf{k}_{\perp}+\mathbf{l}_{\perp}|+|\mathbf{k}_{\perp}|)^{2}\big]\big[\delta_{\mathbf{l}}^{2}+\mathbf{l}_{\perp}^{2}\big]\big[\delta_{-\mathbf{l}}^{2}+\mathbf{l}_{\perp}^{2}\big]}

Feynman diagram BV2-11

From the vertex correction in Tab.
A6
BV2-11, we find a renormalization factor as

\displaystyle\delta\Delta_{b}(11)=-\frac{2g^{4}\Delta_{b}}{N^{2}}\int\frac{d^{d+1}kd^{d+1}l}{(2\pi)^{2d+2}}\frac{\mathcal{N}}{\big[\delta_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{l}}^{2}+(\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L})^{2}\big]\big[\delta_{-\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{L}}^{2}+(\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L})^{2}\big]\big[\delta_{\mathbf{k}}^{2}+\mathbf{K}^{2}\big]\big[\delta_{-\mathbf{L}}^{2}+\mathbf{L}^{2}\big]}D_{1}(k)D_{1}(l),

where
\mathcal{N}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}=\delta_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{l}}\delta_{-\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{l}}\delta_{\mathbf{k}}\delta_{-\mathbf{l}}+\mathbf{K}\cdot\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L})^{2}-(\delta_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{l}}+\delta_{-\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{l}})\delta_{-\mathbf{l}}\mathbf{K}\cdot(\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L})

\displaystyle-(\delta_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{l}}+\delta_{-\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{l}})\delta_{\mathbf{k}}\mathbf{L}\cdot(\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L})-\delta_{\mathbf{k}}\delta_{-\mathbf{l}}(\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L})^{2}-\delta_{\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{l}}\delta_{-\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{l}}\mathbf{K}\cdot\mathbf{L}.

We may ignore
k_{y}
and
l_{y}
in the fermionic part since they would give rise to subleading terms in
g
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\delta\Delta_{b}(11)=-\frac{2g^{4}\Delta_{b}}{N^{2}}\int\frac{d^{d+1}kd^{d+1}l}{(2\pi)^{2d+2}}\frac{\mathcal{N}^{\prime}}{\big[(k_{x}+l_{x})^{2}+(\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L})^{2}\big]^{2}\big[k_{x}^{2}+\mathbf{K}^{2}\big]\big[l_{x}^{2}+\mathbf{L}^{2}\big]}D_{1}(k)D_{1}(l),

where
\mathcal{N}^{\prime}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\prime}=(k_{x}+l_{x})^{2}k_{x}l_{x}+\mathbf{K}\cdot\mathbf{L}(\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L})^{2}+k_{x}l_{x}(\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L})^{2}+(k_{x}+l_{x})^{2}\mathbf{K}\cdot\mathbf{L}.

Integrating over
k_{x}
and
l_{x}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\delta\Delta_{b}(11)=\frac{g^{4}\Delta_{b}}{2N^{2}}\int\frac{d\mathbf{K}dk_{y}d\mathbf{L}dl_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2d}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L}|(|\mathbf{K}|+|\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L}|+|\mathbf{L}|)}\bigg(1-\frac{\mathbf{K}\cdot\mathbf{L}}{|\mathbf{K}||\mathbf{L}|}\bigg)D_{1}(k)D_{1}(l).

Integrating over
k_{y}
and
l_{y}
, we get

\displaystyle\delta\Delta_{b}(11)=\frac{2g^{8/3}\Delta_{b}}{27B_{d}^{2/3}N^{2}}\int\frac{d\mathbf{K}d\mathbf{L}}{(2\pi)^{2d-2}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{K}|^{\frac{d-1}{3}}|\mathbf{L}|^{\frac{d-1}{3}}|\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L}|(|\mathbf{K}|+|\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L}|+|\mathbf{L}|)}\bigg(1-\frac{\mathbf{K}\cdot\mathbf{L}}{|\mathbf{K}||\mathbf{L}|}\bigg).

Introducing coordinates as
\mathbf{K}\cdot\mathbf{L}=KL\cos{\theta}
and scaling
K
as

, we have

\displaystyle\delta\Delta_{b}(11)=\frac{2\Omega^{\prime}g^{8/3}\Delta_{b}}{27B_{d}^{2/3}N^{3}}\int^{\infty}_{|\mathbf{P}|}dLL^{\frac{4d-13}{3}}\int^{\infty}_{0}dkk^{\frac{2d-5}{3}}\int^{\pi}_{0}d\theta\sin^{d-3}{\theta}\frac{1-\cos{\theta}}{\sqrt{1+k^{2}+2k\cos{\theta}}\big(1+k+\sqrt{1+k^{2}+2k\cos{\theta}}\,\big)},

where
\Omega^{\prime}\equiv\frac{4}{(4\pi)^{d-1}\sqrt{\pi}\Gamma(\frac{d-1}{2})\Gamma(\frac{d-2}{2})}
. We find an
\epsilon
pole from the
L
integral as
\int^{\infty}_{|\mathbf{P}|}dLL^{\frac{4d-13}{3}}=\frac{3}{4\epsilon}+\mathcal{O}(1)
. The remaining integral is numerically done as

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{0}dkk^{\frac{2d-5}{3}}\int^{\pi}_{0}d\theta\sin^{d-3}{\theta}\frac{1-\cos{\theta}}{\sqrt{1+k^{2}+2k\cos{\theta}}\big(1+k+\sqrt{1+k^{2}+2k\cos{\theta}}\,\big)}=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\frac{d-2}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{d-1}{2})}(2.264).

As a result, we obtain

\delta\Delta_{b}(11)=(13.58)\Delta_{b}\frac{\tilde{g}^{2}}{\epsilon}.

l_{x}
, we get

\displaystyle\delta\Delta_{b}(17)=\frac{g^{4}\Delta_{b}}{N^{2}}\int\frac{d\mathbf{K}dk_{y}\mathbf{L}dl_{y}}{(2\pi)^{2d}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{L}|(|\mathbf{K}|+|\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L}|+|\mathbf{L}|)}\bigg(1-\frac{(\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L})\cdot\mathbf{K}}{|\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{K}||\mathbf{K}|}\bigg)D_{1}(k)D_{1}(l).

Integrating over
k_{y}
and
l_{y}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\delta\Delta_{b}(17)=\frac{4g^{8/3}\Delta_{b}}{27B_{d}^{2/3}N^{2}}\int\frac{d\mathbf{k}d\mathbf{l}}{(2\pi)^{2d-2}}\frac{1}{|\mathbf{K}|^{\frac{d-1}{3}}|\mathbf{L}|^{\frac{d+2}{3}}(|\mathbf{K}|+|\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L}|+|\mathbf{L}|)}\bigg(1-\frac{(\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L})\cdot\mathbf{K}}{|\mathbf{K}+\mathbf{L}||\mathbf{K}|}\bigg).

Introducing coordinates as
\mathbf{K}\cdot\mathbf{L}=KL\cos{\theta}
and scaling variables as
K=Lk
, we have

\displaystyle\delta\Delta_{b}(17)=\frac{4\Omega^{\prime}g^{8/3}\Delta_{b}}{27B_{d}^{2/3}N^{2}}\int^{\infty}_{|\mathbf{P}|}dLL^{\frac{4d-13}{3}}\int^{\infty}_{0}dkk^{\frac{2d-5}{3}}\int^{\pi}_{0}d\theta\sin^{d-3}{\theta}\frac{1}{1+k+\sqrt{1+k^{2}+2k\cos{\theta}}}\bigg(1-\frac{k+\cos{\theta}}{\sqrt{1+k^{2}+2k\cos{\theta}}}\bigg).

We find an
\epsilon
pole from the
L
integral as
\int^{\infty}_{|\mathbf{P}|}dLL^{\frac{4d-13}{3}}=\frac{3}{4\epsilon}+\mathcal{O}(1)
. The remaining integral are done numerically as

\displaystyle\int^{\infty}_{0}dkk^{\frac{2d-5}{3}}\int^{\pi}_{0}d\theta\sin^{d-3}{\theta}\frac{1}{1+k+\sqrt{1+k^{2}+2k\cos{\theta}}}\bigg(1-\frac{k+\cos{\theta}}{\sqrt{1+k^{2}+2k\cos{\theta}}}\bigg)=\frac{\sqrt{\pi}\,\Gamma(\frac{d-2}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{d-1}{2})}(0.4213).

The remaining integration is the same with
\delta\Delta_{b}(11)
. As a result, we obtain

\delta\Delta_{b}(17)=(5.056)\Delta_{b}\frac{\tilde{g}^{2}}{\epsilon}.

(D13)

E
Derivation of critical exponents

1
Critical exponents for fermion and boson fields

Here, we derive the Callan-Symanzik equations and define the critical exponents for the correlation functions. We first consider the following correlation function:

G^{(m,n)}(\{k_{i}\},\mathbf{F})\equiv\left\langle\bar{\Psi}(k_{1})\cdots\Psi(k_{m})\Phi(k_{m+1})\cdots\right\rangle,

(E1)

where
\mathbf{F}=(\tilde{g},\tilde{\Delta}_{f},\tilde{\Delta}_{b})
. In a renormalized theory, the correlation function is expressed as

G^{(m,n)}_{ren.}(\{k_{i,r}\},\mu,\mathbf{F}_{r})=\mu^{-[G]}Z_{\Psi}^{-m/2}Z_{\Phi}^{-n/2}G^{(m,n)}_{bare}(\{k_{i}\},\mathbf{F}),

(E2)

where
G^{(m,n)}_{ren.}(\{k_{i,r}\},\mu,\mathbf{F}_{r})
and
G^{(m,n)}_{bare}(\{k_{i}\},\mathbf{F})
represent the renormalized and bare correlation functions, respectively. Here,
\{k_{i,r}\}
are scaled momenta,
\mathbf{F}_{r}
are renormalized coupling constants, and
[G]=m[\Psi]+n[\Phi]+z+\bar{z}(d-2)+\frac{3}{2}
. Using the fact
\frac{dG^{(m,n)}_{bare}}{d\mu}=0
, we obtain the following Callan-Symanzik equation for
G^{(m,n)}_{ren.}(\{k_{i,r}\},\mu,\mathbf{F}_{r})
:

\bigg[\sum_{i}k_{i}\cdot\bm{\nabla}_{k_{i}}-\mathbf{\beta}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot\mathbf{\nabla}_{\mathbf{F}}-m\big([\Psi]+\gamma_{{}_{\Psi}}\big)-n\big([\Phi]+\gamma_{{}_{\Phi}}\big)-D_{sc}\bigg]G^{(m,n)}_{ren.}(\{k_{i}\},\mu,\mathbf{F})=0,

(E3)

where the derivative expressions are defined as
k\cdot\mathbf{\nabla}_{k}\equiv zk_{0}\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{0}}+\bar{z}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{\nabla}_{\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}+\delta_{\mathbf{k}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\delta_{\mathbf{k}}}
and
\nabla_{\mathbf{F}}\equiv(\frac{2}{3}\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{g}},\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\Delta}_{f}},\frac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{\Delta}_{b}})
, and the beta function is written in the vector form as
\mathbf{\beta}_{\mathbf{F}}\equiv(\beta_{\tilde{g}},\beta_{\tilde{\Delta}_{f}},\beta_{\tilde{\Delta}_{b}})
, and
D_{sc}=z+\bar{z}(d-2)+\frac{3}{2}
. The critical exponents in the Callan-Symanzik equations are defined as

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\partial\ln(Z_{0}/Z_{2})}{\partial\ln\mu},

\displaystyle\bar{z}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\partial\ln(Z_{1}/Z_{2})}{\partial\ln\mu},

\displaystyle\gamma_{{}_{\Psi}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\ln Z_{2}}{\partial\ln\mu},

\displaystyle\gamma_{{}_{\Phi}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\ln Z_{3}}{\partial\ln\mu},

(E4)

where
Z_{0,1,2,3}
are the renormalization factors that relate the bare and scaled momenta as

\displaystyle k_{0,r}=\mu\frac{Z_{0}}{Z_{2}}k_{0},~\mathbf{k}_{\perp,r}=\mu\frac{Z_{1}}{Z_{2}}\mathbf{k}_{\perp},k_{x,r}=\mu k_{x},~k_{y,r}=\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}k_{y}.

(E5)

We next consider another type of correlation function given by

G^{(m,n)}(\{k_{i}\},\{\gamma_{\mu(j)}\},\mathbf{F})\equiv\left\langle\bar{\Psi}(k_{1})\gamma_{\mu(1)}\Psi(k_{2})\cdots\bar{\Psi}(k_{m-1})\gamma_{\mu(l)}\Psi(k_{m})\phi(k_{m+1})\cdots\right\rangle,

(E6)

where
\gamma_{\mu(j)}
represent the gamma matrices for the corresponding coupling constants, i.e.
\gamma_{d-1}
for
\tilde{g}
and
\tilde{\Delta}_{f}
or the identity matrix
I_{2}
for
\tilde{\Delta}_{b}
. With a similar consideration for
G^{(m,n)}(\{k_{i}\},\mathbf{F})
, we obtain the Callan-Symanzik equation for
G^{(m,n)}(\{k_{i}\},\{\gamma_{\mu(j)}\},\mathbf{F})
as

\Bigg[\sum_{i}k_{i}\cdot\mathbf{\nabla}_{k_{i}}-\mathbf{\beta}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot\mathbf{\nabla}_{\mathbf{F}}-m\big([\Psi]+\gamma_{{}_{\Psi}}\big)-n\big([\Phi]+\gamma_{{}_{\Phi}}\big)-D_{sc}+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\gamma_{\mu(j)}^{ver}\Bigg]G^{(n,m)}(\{k_{i}\},\{\gamma_{\mu(j)}\},\mu,\mathbf{F})=0.

(E7)

Here,
\gamma_{\mu(j)}^{ver}

Z_{0,1,2,3}
are the anomalous dimension of the coupling constants are defined as

\displaystyle\gamma_{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\ln Z_{g}}{\partial\ln\mu},

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Delta_{f}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\ln Z_{\Delta_{f}}}{\partial\ln\mu},

\displaystyle\gamma_{\Delta_{b}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\ln Z_{\Delta_{f}}}{\partial\ln\mu},

(E8)

where
Z_{g}
,
Z_{\Delta_{b}}
, and
Z_{\Delta_{f}}
are the renormalization factors that relate the bare and renormalized coupling constants as

\displaystyle Z_{g}g

\displaystyle=\mu^{-\frac{\epsilon}{2}}(Z_{0}/Z_{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}(Z_{1}/Z_{2})^{\frac{d-2}{2}}Z_{2}Z_{3}^{\frac{1}{2}}g_{0},

\displaystyle Z_{\Delta_{f}}\Delta_{f}

\displaystyle=\mu^{-\epsilon}(Z_{1}/Z_{2})^{d-2}Z_{2}^{2}\Delta_{f,0},

\displaystyle Z_{\Delta_{b}}\Delta_{b}

\displaystyle=\mu^{-\epsilon}(Z_{1}/Z_{2})^{d-2}Z_{2}^{2}\Delta_{b,0}.

(E9)

We finally compute the critical exponents for the correlation functions, which are defined in Eqs. (
E4
) and (
E8
), which are given in Eqs. (17) and (18) in the main text. We note that in the epsilon expansion the renormalization factors are given in the following forms:

\displaystyle Z_{0}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{A_{0}}{\epsilon}+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg),

\displaystyle Z_{1}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{A_{1}}{\epsilon}+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg),

\displaystyle Z_{2}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{A_{2}}{\epsilon}+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg),

\displaystyle Z_{3}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{A_{3}}{\epsilon}+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg),

\displaystyle Z_{g}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{A_{0}}{\epsilon}+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg),

\displaystyle Z_{{}_{\Delta_{f}}}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{A_{{}_{\Delta_{f}}}}{\epsilon}+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg),

\displaystyle Z_{{}_{\Delta_{b}}}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{A_{{}_{\Delta_{b}}}}{\epsilon}+\mathcal{O}\bigg(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg).

(E10)

Inserting Eq. (
E10
) into Eqs. (
E4
) and (
E8
) solving the resulting equations order by order in
\epsilon
, we obtain the following expressions:

\displaystyle\bar{z}

\displaystyle=\big(1+\mathbf{F}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{F}}(A_{1}-A_{2})\big)^{-1},

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=\bar{z}\big(1-\mathbf{F}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{F}}(A_{0}-A_{1})\big),

\displaystyle\gamma_{{}_{\Psi}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\bar{z}\mathbf{F}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{F}}A_{2},

\displaystyle\gamma_{{}_{\Phi}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\bar{z}\mathbf{F}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{F}}A_{3},

\displaystyle\gamma_{g}

\displaystyle=-\bar{z}\mathbf{F}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{F}}A_{g},

\displaystyle\gamma_{{}_{\Delta_{f}}}

\displaystyle=-\bar{z}\mathbf{F}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{F}}A_{\Delta_{f}},

\displaystyle\gamma_{{}_{\Delta_{b}}}

\displaystyle=-\bar{z}\mathbf{F}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{F}}A_{\Delta_{b}},

(E11)

To obtain Eq. (
E11
), we should note that the coupling constants have the
\mu
-factors in front of them as
\mu^{\frac{2}{3}\epsilon}\tilde{g}
,
\mu^{\frac{2}{3}\epsilon}\tilde{\Delta}_{f}
, and
\mu^{\epsilon}\tilde{\Delta}_{b}
, which we have ignored in the loop correction computations, for simplicity. To find
A_{0}
,
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
,
A_{3}
,
A_{g}
,
A_{\Delta_{f}}
, and
A_{\Delta_{b}}
, we gather all corresponding contributions from Sec.
A
,
C
,
B
, and
D
. As a result, we obtain

\displaystyle A_{0}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{g}-\tilde{\Delta}_{f}-\tilde{\Delta}_{f}-0.54\tilde{g}^{2}-0.45\tilde{\Delta}_{f}\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{g}}{N}}-0.45\tilde{\Delta}_{b}\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{g}}{N}},

\displaystyle A_{1}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{g}-0.54\tilde{g}^{2}-16\tilde{\Delta}_{f}\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{g}}{N}}-16\tilde{\Delta}_{b}\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{g}}{N}},

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=-0.5\tilde{\Delta}_{f}-0.5\tilde{\Delta}_{f}-0.11\tilde{g}^{2},

\displaystyle A_{\Delta_{f}}

\displaystyle=-\tilde{\Delta}_{f}-\tilde{\Delta}_{b}-0.75\frac{\tilde{\Delta}_{b}^{2}}{\tilde{\Delta}_{f}}-0.23\tilde{g}^{2}+2.1\tilde{g}\tilde{\Delta}_{f}-4.2\tilde{\Delta}_{f}\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{g}}{N}}-4.2\frac{\tilde{\Delta}_{b}^{2}}{\tilde{\Delta}_{f}}\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{g}}{N}},

\displaystyle A_{\Delta_{b}}

\displaystyle=6\tilde{g}+0.14\tilde{\Delta}_{f}+18\tilde{g}^{2}+1.4\tilde{g}\tilde{\Delta}_{f}+8.3\tilde{\Delta}_{b}\sqrt{\frac{\tilde{g}}{N}},

(E12)

where
A_{g}=A_{2}
and
A_{3}=0
. Inserting Eq. (
E12
) into Eq. (
E11
), we obtain the critical exponents in Eqs. (17) and (18) in the main text.

2
Critical exponents for thermodynamic quantities

Here, we explicitly compute the critical exponents for thermodynamic quantities, which are given in Eq. (37) in the main text with a heuristic argument. We start with the order parameter
m
for the Ising-nematic order, which is defined as,

\displaystyle m

\displaystyle\equiv-\left.\frac{\partial f}{\partial h}\right|_{h\rightarrow 0}=\left\langle\Phi(x)+N(x)\right\rangle=m^{(1)}+m^{(2)},

(E13)

where
m^{(1)}
and
m^{(2)}
represent the fermion and boson contribution given as

\displaystyle m^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\left\langle\phi(x)\right\rangle,

\displaystyle m^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\left\langle j(x)\right\rangle=\int\frac{d^{D}k}{(2\pi)^{D}}\textrm{tr}[\gamma_{d-1}G(k)].

(E14)

By solving the Callan-Symanzik equation for
m^{(1)}
, which is given by

\bigg[\mu\partial_{\mu}+\mathbf{\beta}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot\mathbf{\nabla}_{\mathbf{F}}+\frac{1}{2}(D-1+\gamma_{{}_{\Phi}})\bigg]m^{(1)}=0,

(E15)

we obtain the scaling behavior of
m^{(1)}
as

m^{(1)}\sim\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}(D-1+\gamma_{{}_{\Phi}})}\sim(-r)^{\frac{\nu}{2}(D-1+\gamma_{{}_{\Phi}})}.

(E16)

To find the scaling behavior of
m^{(2)}
, we consider the Callan-Symanzik equations for a fermion Green’s function
G(k)=\left\langle\Psi(k)\bar{\Psi}(k)\right\rangle
, which is given by

\Big[k\cdot\mathbf{\nabla}_{k}-\mathbf{\beta}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot\mathbf{\nabla}_{\mathbf{F}}+1-\gamma_{{}_{\Psi}}\Big]G(k,\mu,\mathbf{F})=0.

(E17)

The solution is represented as

G(k,\mu,\mathbf{F})=\frac{1}{\mu^{\gamma_{{}_{\Psi}}}|\delta_{\mathbf{k}}|^{1-\gamma_{{}_{\Psi}}}}g(k_{0}/|\delta_{\mathbf{k}}|^{z}).

(E18)

Using this expression, we find the scaling behavior of
m^{(2)}
as

\displaystyle m^{(2)}\sim\int\frac{d^{D}k}{(2\pi)^{D}}\frac{1}{|\delta_{\mathbf{k}}|^{1-\gamma_{{}_{\Psi}}}}g\bigg(\frac{k_{0}}{|\delta_{\mathbf{k}}|^{z}},\frac{r}{|\delta_{\mathbf{k}}|^{1/\nu}}\bigg)\sim(-r)^{\nu(D-1+\gamma_{{}_{\Psi}})}.

(E19)

Using the values of
D=5/2
,
\gamma_{{}_{\Phi}}=0
, and
\gamma_{{}_{\Psi}}=0.24
at the DNFL fixed point, we obtain

m^{(1)}\sim(-r)^{\nu},~~~m^{(2)}\sim(-r)^{1.97\nu}.

(E20)

We note that the bosonic contribution
m^{(1)}
is much larger than the fermionic contribution
m^{(2)}
near the critical point
r\approx 0
. This observation justifies ignoring the coupling of the external field with fermionic excitations.

We next compute the susceptibility for Ising-nematic order parameter, which is defined as

\displaystyle\chi

\displaystyle\equiv\left.\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial h^{2}}\right|_{h\rightarrow 0}=\int d^{D}x\left\langle\Phi(x)\Phi(0)+N(x)N(0)\right\rangle=\chi^{(1)}+\chi^{(2)},

(E21)

where
\chi^{(1)}
and
\chi^{(2)}
represent the fermion and boson contribution given as

\displaystyle\chi^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\int d^{D}x\left\langle\phi(x)\phi(0)\right\rangle=\lim_{k\rightarrow 0}D(k),

\displaystyle\chi^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\int d^{D}x\left\langle j(x)j(0)\right\rangle=\int\frac{d^{D}k}{(2\pi)^{D}}\left\langle j(k)j(-k)\right\rangle.

(E22)

To find the scaling behavior of
\chi^{(1)}
, we consider the Callan-Symanzik equations for a boson Green’s function
D(k)=\left\langle\phi(k)\phi(-k)\right\rangle
, which is given by

\Big[k\cdot\mathbf{\nabla}_{k}-\mathbf{\beta}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot\mathbf{\nabla}_{\mathbf{F}}+1-\gamma_{{}_{\Phi}}\Big]D(k,\mu,\mathbf{F})=0.

(E23)

The solution is represented as

D(k,\mu,\mathbf{F})=\frac{1}{\mu^{\gamma_{{}_{\Phi}}}|k_{y}|^{2(1-\gamma_{{}_{\Phi}})}}d(k_{0}/|k_{y}|^{2z}).

(E24)

Using this expression, we find the scaling behavior of
\chi^{(1)}
as

\chi^{(1)}=\lim_{k\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{|k_{y}|^{2(1-\gamma_{{}_{\Phi}})}}d\bigg(\frac{k_{0}}{|k_{y}|^{2z}},\frac{r}{|k_{y}|^{2/\nu}}\bigg)\sim|r|^{-\nu(1-\gamma_{{}_{\Phi}})}.

(E25)

To find the scaling behavior of
\chi^{(2)}
, we consider the Callan-Symanzik equations for the correlation function
G^{(2)}(k)=\left\langle j(k)j(-k)\right\rangle
, which is given by

\Big[k\cdot\mathbf{\nabla}_{k}-\mathbf{\beta}_{\mathbf{F}}\cdot\mathbf{\nabla}_{\mathbf{F}}+2+\gamma_{{}_{\Delta_{f}}}^{ms}\Big]G^{(2)}(k)=0,

(E26)

where
\gamma_{{}_{\Delta_{f}}}^{ms}=\gamma_{{}_{\Delta_{f}}}-2\gamma_{{}_{\Psi}}
. The solution is represented as

G^{(2)}(k)=\frac{1}{|\delta_{\mathbf{k}}|^{2+\gamma_{{}_{\Delta_{f}}}^{ms}}}g^{(2)}\bigg(\frac{k_{0}}{|\delta_{\mathbf{k}}|^{z}},\frac{r}{|\delta_{\mathbf{k}}|^{1/\nu}}\bigg).

(E27)

Using this expression, we find the scaling behavior of
\chi^{(2)}
as

\chi^{(2)}=\int\frac{d^{D}k}{(2\pi)^{D}}\frac{1}{|\delta_{\mathbf{k}}|^{2+\gamma_{{}_{\Delta_{f}}}^{ms}}}g^{(2)}\bigg(\frac{k_{0}}{|\delta_{\mathbf{k}}|^{z}},\frac{r}{|\delta_{\mathbf{k}}|^{1/\nu}}\bigg)\sim|r|^{\nu(D-2-\gamma_{{}_{\Delta_{f}}}^{ms})}.

(E28)

Using the value of
\gamma_{\Delta_{f}}^{ms}=-0.50
at the DNFL fixed point, we find

\chi^{(1)}\sim|r|^{-\nu},~~\chi^{(2)}\sim|r|^{\nu}.

(E29)

We note that the bosonic contribution
\chi^{(1)}
is again much larger than the fermionic contribution
\chi^{(2)}
near the critical point
r\approx 0
. This observation again justifies ignoring the coupling of the external field with fermionic excitations.

F
Ward identity

The effective field theory of Eq. (4) has a U(1) symmetry, given by
\Psi_{j}(k)\rightarrow e^{i\theta_{v}}\Psi_{j}(k)
. Associated with this symmetry, we derive the Schwinger-Dyson equation for
\langle\psi(x)\bar{\psi}(0)\rangle
and find the following identity

\displaystyle\Gamma_{d-1}(p,0)=\frac{\partial G^{-1}(p)}{\partial p_{x}},

(F1)

where
\Gamma_{d-1}(p+q,q)
is the irreducible vertex function resulting from
\langle j_{d-1}(x^{\prime})\psi(x)\bar{\psi}(0)\rangle
, and
G(p)
is the fully renormalized fermion propagator.
j_{d-1}\equiv\bar{\psi}\gamma_{d-1}\psi
is the conserved current related to the U(1) symmetry in the
(d-1)
direction. The Ward identity of Eq. (
F1
) implies that the vertex function for
\gamma_{d-1}
and the fermion kinetic energy should be renormalized at the same rate. For the fermion-boson Yukawa coupling, where bosons are coupled to
j_{d-1}
conserved currents, this equation implies the following relation

\displaystyle\gamma_{g}=2\gamma_{\psi},

(F2)

which should be preserved in all loop corrections.

There is a similar identity for forward disorder scattering. To figure it out, we define
\gamma_{{}_{\Delta_{f}}}^{ss}\equiv\gamma_{{}_{\Delta_{f}}}-\gamma_{{}_{\Delta_{f}}}^{ms}
, where
\gamma_{{}_{\Delta_{f}}}^{ss}
(
\gamma_{{}_{\Delta_{f}}}^{ms}
) is the anomalous dimension involved with a single (multiple) scattering process. For example, in Tab.
A3
, the Feynman diagrams labeled as “FV1-3”, “FV1-5”, and “FV1-6” fall into the single scattering process while those labeled as “FV1-1”, “FV1-2” , and “FV1-4” fall into the multiple scattering process. Only
\gamma_{{}_{\Delta_{f}}}^{ss}
is subject to the Ward identity because the forward scattering acts effectively as a vertex function for
\gamma_{d-1}
only in the single scattering process. Then, the Ward identity in Eq. (
F1
) implies another relation,

\displaystyle\gamma_{{}_{\Delta_{f}}}^{ss}=4\gamma_{\psi},

(F3)

which should be preserved in all loop corrections.
